And then I get angry again—at the driver, at the town—for letting it happen.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend’s death, have you noticed a constant low mood or loss of interest in activities you used to enjoy, even outside of thinking about what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I used to really get into planning the garden beds, and quilting with the ladies… even book club, the theater. Now I see them written down and I just feel almost nothing. Like those things belonged to who I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt unusually tired or noticed that you don’t have the energy to do things you normally could since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I’m older, so I get tired sometimes anyway. I haven’t paid much attention to whether it’s different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. When I try to go over the senior-center budget, or even just read a program schedule, the numbers and dates sort of run together. I have to check the same simple thing again and again, like my mind won’t quite hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since your friend's death? Like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything clear with that. My appetite’s maybe… about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t think my weight has changed much, but I haven’t been watching it closely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a bad person or felt excessive guilt, even beyond what happened with your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I mean, I’ve had thoughts about what happened, but I wouldn’t say I feel like I’m a bad person beyond that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like they were for a different version of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like gardening or quilting, don’t bring you any pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to really get into planning the garden beds, and quilting with the ladies… even book club, the theater. Now I see them written down and I just feel almost nothing. Like those things belonged to who I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt unusually tired or noticed that you don’t have the energy to do things you normally could since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I’m older, so I get tired sometimes anyway. I haven’t paid much attention to whether it’s different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. When I try to go over the senior-center budget, or even just read a program schedule, the numbers and dates sort of run together. I have to check the same simple thing again and again, like my mind won’t quite hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since your friend's death? Like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything clear with that. My appetite’s maybe… about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t think my weight has changed much, but I haven’t been watching it closely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a bad person or felt excessive guilt, even beyond what happened with your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I mean, I’ve had thoughts about what happened, but I wouldn’t say I feel like I’m a bad person beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since your friend's death, like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t kept track of it closely. Some nights are better than others, I suppose, but I don’t know that I’d call it a big change.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend's death, even beyond specific moments of sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it’s most of the day nearly every day. I have sad moments, of course, but I’m not sure I’d call it depressed like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, your mood has been down or depressed, rather than just having occasional sad moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve had thoughts about what happened, but I wouldn’t say I feel like I’m a bad person beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since your friend's death, like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t kept track of it closely. I have sad moments, of course, but I’m not sure I’d call it depressed like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, your mood has been down or depressed, rather than just having occasional sad moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say that, no. I have times when I feel sad, but… not most of the day most days, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, your mood has been down or depressed, rather than just having occasional sad moments?\n\nPatient: I… I think I already sort of answered that. I don’t know if I’d call it just “depressed,” exactly. It’s more this heavy mix of guilt and fear and anger that hasn’t really let up since she was killed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced days where it was hard to get out of bed, or you felt like nothing mattered anymore?\n\nPatient: Some days, yes. I’ll see things on the calendar—gardening, quilting, book club, even the theater—and I just… don’t feel much about them anymore. Like they were for a different version of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like gardening or quilting, don’t bring you any pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to really get into planning the garden beds, and quilting with the ladies… even book club, the theater. Now I see them written down and I just feel almost nothing. Like those things belonged to who I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt unusually tired or noticed that you don’t have the energy to do things you normally could since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I’m older, so I get tired sometimes anyway. I haven’t paid much attention to whether it’s different.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. When I try to go over the senior-center budget, or even just read a program schedule, the numbers and dates sort of run together.
